## Preface

A system that works on the day it ships has passed only the first and easiest test. The harder test begins the morning after, when the system meets production: real users in numbers nobody quite forecast, dependencies that slow down without warning, changes made by forty teams at once, adversaries who probe it every night, and a calendar that sooner or later delivers the busiest hour of the year. Some systems bend under that pressure and recover; others break, and break in ways that spread. The difference is rarely luck. It is usually the accumulated result of many engineering decisions, most of them made long before the bad hour, about what to promise, how much capacity to buy, how to change the system safely, where to draw the walls that contain failure, how to keep data that cannot be redeployed, and how to defend the whole against people who wish it harm.

I call that body of decisions **production engineering**: the work of designing, measuring, changing and protecting a system so that it withstands production. This book is about that work. It begins with reliability as a product decision, the choice of how much unreliability users can absorb, expressed as service level objectives and spent as an error budget. It turns to performance, capacity and cost, the arithmetic of how much system a promise requires. It follows the runtime and its control plane, the crowd of loops that keep a cloud system in the shape we asked for, and then the delivery pipeline through which every change must pass. It takes up the stability patterns and experiments that keep a failure small, the data and state that make recovery hard, and the security operations that keep defenses current. And it ends with AI workloads, the newest components in production and in some ways the strangest, before a capstone that brings every chapter to bear on one system at once.

None of these techniques is as new as its tooling. Erlang worked out in the 1910s how many lines a telephone exchange needs; Maxwell explained in 1868 why a steam engine's governor could make the engine hunt; the Bell System chose its nines and engineered to them; AT&T's network showed in 1990 how a recovery path can carry a failure from switch to switch. The tools have changed almost beyond recognition, and the lessons beneath them have hardly changed at all. I have come to believe that the newest techniques are best understood, and best used, by someone who knows the older lessons that explain why they work, because a team that has the tool without the reason will misuse the tool the first time the situation departs from the tutorial. So each chapter roots today's practice in its lineage. AI appears where it really changes the practice, and nowhere else.

This is the third book in a series. The first, *Software Architecture in the AI & Cloud Era*, which this book calls the Architecture book, asks how to shape a system; the second, *Building Products & Platforms*, is still in preparation. This one asks how to engineer that system so that it withstands production. *Operating Production Systems in the AI & Cloud Era*, the Operations book, follows it and asks what happens when production fails anyway: how complex systems fail, how people and automation respond in the moment, and how organizations learn from what went wrong. Each book stands on its own, but they share one company and one story, and they were written to be read together.

### Influences & Attribution

Much of this book's method comes from others, and I want to be plain about where. Each chapter's references point to the original works, which are always the better read; this book's job is to connect them and show them working together at one company. Service level objectives follow Google's *Site Reliability Engineering* and *The Site Reliability Workbook*, Alex Hidalgo's *Implementing Service Level Objectives*, and Ben Treynor and colleagues' "The Calculus of Service Availability". Performance and capacity draw on Brendan Gregg's *Systems Performance*, Neil Gunther's *Guerrilla Capacity Planning*, Arun Kejariwal and John Allspaw's *The Art of Capacity Planning*, and J.R. Storment and Mike Fuller's *Cloud FinOps*. The runtime chapter leans on Kief Morris's *Infrastructure as Code*, Bilgin Ibryam and Roland Huß's *Kubernetes Patterns*, and Camille Fournier and Ian Nowland's *Platform Engineering*. Delivery draws on Jez Humble and David Farley's *Continuous Delivery* and on the DORA research of Nicole Forsgren and colleagues. Stability patterns come from Michael Nygard's *Release It!*, deepened by Marc Brooker's writing on retries and backoff and by Nathan Bronson and colleagues' work on metastable failures; chaos engineering follows the principles published by its Netflix originators and Kripa Krishnan's account of Google's disaster-recovery testing. Data and recovery draw on Martin Kleppmann and Chris Riccomini's *Designing Data-Intensive Applications*, security on *Building Secure and Reliable Systems* by Heather Adkins and colleagues and on Kelly Shortridge and Aaron Rinehart's *Security Chaos Engineering*, and the operation of AI systems on Shreya Shankar and Hamel Husain's work on evals and Chip Huyen's *AI Engineering*.

The book's older foundations come from engineers and scientists who never ran a cloud service: Agner Krarup Erlang on telephone traffic, James Clerk Maxwell on governors, Walter Shewhart on control charts, Jim Gray on why computers stop, Eugene Spafford on the Morris worm, and Joseph Weizenbaum on the trust people lend to fluent machines. The public post-incident reports of Amazon Web Services, Google Cloud, Cloudflare, CrowdStrike, GitLab and GitHub, and the SEC's account of Knight Capital, supply the book's real-world cases, and they are described here as history from the public record.

Several terms used in this book as common vocabulary were coined by specific people or groups, and they deserve the credit: error budgets (Google SRE), the USE method (Brendan Gregg), the RED method (Tom Wilkie), the Universal Scalability Law (Neil Gunther), metastable failures (Nathan Bronson and colleagues), shuffle sharding (Amazon Web Services), chaos engineering (Netflix) and the confused deputy (Norm Hardy).

### Authorship & Disclaimer

This book was written and directed by Oleksiy Pylypenko, with AI assistance (Anthropic's Claude). The judgment in it comes from twenty-plus years of practice: platform engineering inside a large bank, API architecture in fintech, and a decade of maintaining a widely used open-source library. The author chose the thesis, the structure, the examples and the trade-offs, edited every chapter, and takes responsibility for every claim, errors included. Encore and every kata company are fictional, and historical events are described from the public record. The content is provided as-is, without warranty of any kind. It is educational material, not professional advice for any specific system, and the license's full disclaimer of warranties applies.

## How to Read This Book

One company runs through all nine chapters. **Encore** sells tickets to live concerts, and its traffic can rise 500× in minutes when a major tour is announced, which makes it a demanding place to learn how to engineer for production. Readers of the Architecture book watched Encore grow from nine engineers to 400; this book picks the company up the morning after that story ended. Chapter 1 introduces Encore for readers meeting it for the first time, with a map of its services and of where they run, each later chapter adds the parts of the company it needs, and an appendix gathers all of them on one page for reference. One Friday sits on the book's horizon: the on-sale for a fictional artist named Vesper Hale. This book prepares for it, chapter by chapter, and points out the small, reasonable decisions that will matter on the day. The Operations book tells the morning itself and what Encore learned from it.

Each chapter closes with a **kata**, a practice brief for a different company with its own scale and constraints, together with a rubric to check your work against. The word comes from the martial arts, where a kata is a set form practiced until it becomes second nature, and the idea is much the same as a musician's scales: the value lies in repetition in a place where mistakes are cheap, so that the moves are familiar when mistakes become expensive. Every section ends with a short recap and an exercise. The exercises work best done against a system you actually run, because the point is to see the idea in your own production rather than in Encore's. Each chapter's references list the field's standard works, and those marked *free online* cost nothing to read.

The chapters build on one another, but each can be read on its own. A reader setting objectives for a new service may start with Chapter 1, a reader facing a peak may start with Chapter 2, and a reader whose system keeps falling over in interesting ways may start with Chapter 5. Chapter 1 is the one I would ask every reader to make time for, because the objectives it defines are the yardstick every later chapter measures against.

## The Arc

The nine chapters fall into four parts. They move from deciding what to promise, through running and changing the system that keeps the promise, to withstanding the failures that will test it, and they end with AI workloads and a capstone that applies the whole book at once.

**Promises and capacity (Chapters 1–2):** reliability as a product decision, and performance, capacity and cost.

**Running and changing (Chapters 3–4):** the runtime and its control plane, and shipping change safely.
{: .flush}

**Withstanding failure (Chapters 5–7):** designing for failure, data and disaster recovery, and security operations.
{: .flush}

**The destination (Chapters 8–9):** operating AI workloads, and the capstone.
{: .flush}
