## Appendix: Encore at a Glance {#encore-glance}

Throughout this book, Encore has been introduced the way a touring crew learns a new venue: one part at a time, as each part becomes relevant. Seat Inventory appears when a chapter needs a core domain to protect, the Sale Gate when a chapter needs a door to control, and the payment providers when a chapter needs something outside our walls to fail. That arrangement serves a reader who goes from front to back, because every part arrives with its reason for existing. It serves much less well the reader who opens the book at Chapter 5, or who returns months later to look up one practice and finds that the example assumes things they never read or have since forgotten.

This appendix is for that reader. It gathers the whole company onto one page, so that a single glance can answer the question "what was that again?" Each row names one part of Encore, says in a sentence or two what it is, and in nearly every case points in two directions. The first pointer leads to the Architecture book, where the part was designed and where the reasoning behind its shape is laid out. The second leads to the chapter of this book that shows how the part is engineered to withstand production, and, where the part's story is one of incidents and the people who respond to them, to the Operations book as well. The books divide the labor deliberately: the Architecture book explains why Encore looks the way it does, this one explains what it takes, on-sale after on-sale, to keep it working that way, and the Operations book follows what happens on the days it does not.

A glossary like this is a small exercise in the abstraction the rest of the book keeps recommending. Every row compresses a chapter's worth of reasoning into a line, and the compression is useful only because the full explanation sits one link away. Read the table as a map of the territory, then follow a pointer when you need to walk the ground itself.

| Term | What it is | Where to read more |
|---|---|---|
| **Encore** | A company selling concert tickets across the EU, priced in euros, which also sells its platform to venues as software as a service (SaaS). Three facts shape everything: traffic rises 500× within minutes when a tour goes on sale, every seat must be sold exactly once, and bots arrive ahead of the fans | [Architecture book, 1.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#encore-our-running-example); Chapter 1 |
| **Organization** | 400 engineers in 40 stream-aligned teams, each of which builds and runs its own services, plus a platform group that makes up 5–10% of engineering. Each team carries its own on-call, and a single incident commander rotation draws on trained engineers, most of them senior | [Architecture book, 5.5](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#conways-law-is-a-design-tool); Chapter 1; Operations book, Chapter 7 |
| **Customers** | Four kinds: fans, event organizers, partners calling the public API at `api.encore.com`, and SaaS tenants | [Architecture book, 7.3](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-gateway-earns-its-place-at-the-edge); Chapters 1 and 7 |
| **On-sale** | The scheduled moment when a tour's tickets go on sale, usually 10:00 CET on a Friday | [Architecture book, 1.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#encore-our-running-example); Chapters 1 and 2 |
| **Sale Gate** | The informal name for On-Sale Admission: the virtual waiting room and admission controller that stands in front of the purchase path. It runs on a serverless edge, is pre-warmed before a scheduled on-sale, and scales to 400 instances | [Architecture book, 2.4](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#24-choosing-a-style); Chapters 3 and 5 |
| **Admission rate** | How many fans per second, per event, the Gate lets through to buy: about 1,200. The figure is sized so that Seat Inventory stays below 80% of its load capacity | [Architecture book, 4.5](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#45-degradation-and-chaos); Chapters 2 and 5; Operations book, Chapter 5 |
| **Bot Screening** | The service that decides whether each session belongs to a fan or a bot, using signals rented from the edge provider together with velocity rules. Its risk data lives in a cloud account of its own | [Architecture book, 10.4](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#blast-radius-is-a-design-variable); Chapters 1 and 7 |
| **Seat Inventory** | The core domain and the sole authority on whether a seat is free. It is event-sourced and uses command–query responsibility segregation (CQRS), so the model that records sales is separate from the models that answer questions about them | [Architecture book, 3.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#31-strategic-design), [6.2](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#cqrs-two-models-one-truth); Chapters 2 and 6 |
| **Hold** | A seat reserved for one fan for 5 minutes, created by `POST /holds`. If checkout does not finish in that time, the hold is released automatically and the seat returns to sale | [Architecture book, 3.2](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#aggregates-consistency-has-a-size); Chapter 6 |
| **Shard and home region** | A large event's seats are split by `event_id` into four shards. Each shard has a fenced leader that handles about 10,000 reads and 1,500 ordered writes a second. All of an event's writes are pinned to one region, its home region | [Architecture book, 4.2](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#42-state--replication-partitioning-consistency); Chapters 2 and 6 |
| **Orders & Payments saga** | The orchestrated purchase, in which each step is paired with a compensation that undoes it, all within a 3 s checkout budget. If the primary payment service provider (PSP) fails, new payments go to the secondary. If both are degraded, verified fans' seats are held pending payment for up to 20 minutes. Whatever happens, no ticket is issued without an authorized payment | [Architecture book, 5.2](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-saga-transactions-without-the-transaction); Chapters 1 and 5 |
| **PSP** | An external payment service provider. Encore integrates two, routes each new payment to a healthy one, and keeps both behind an anti-corruption layer. The PSP call takes about 800 ms of the checkout budget. Encore holds no card numbers | [Architecture book, 4.4](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#anatomy-of-a-self-inflicted-outage); Chapters 1 and 5; Operations book, Chapter 2 |
| **The first big outage** | The PSP slowed from 200 ms to 8 s; a retry storm followed, and autoscaling made it worse by adding more callers. It was fixed with retry budgets and a single layer responsible for retries | [Architecture book, 4.4](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#anatomy-of-a-self-inflicted-outage); Chapter 5; Operations book, Chapter 2 |
| **Finance ledger** | The relational record of money, fed by `SaleCompleted` events, reconciled daily and closed nightly | [Architecture book, 9.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#91-data-ownership-in-distributed-systems); Chapter 6 |
| **Seat-map geometry** | The physical shape of a hall, served by Catalog from a cache. Geometry changes rarely and can be cached; availability changes constantly and is never cached | [Architecture book, 1.3](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#13-modularity-and-logical-components); Chapters 2 and 6; Operations book, Chapter 6 |
| **Notifications** | The service that delivers tickets by email and to phone wallets. A coding agent rebuilt it overnight at the end of the Architecture book; Chapter 4 revisits that change, and the Operations book opens on the morning after it | [Architecture book, 15.4](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-inversion-the-preface-promised); Chapter 4; Operations book, Chapter 1 |
| **`ticket-sales.v2`** | The event stream that carries `SeatSold`, `TicketIssued`, `SaleCompleted` and related events to every consumer | [Architecture book, 6.5](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#65-flow--streams-as-products); Operations book, Chapter 4 |
| **Tenants and cells** | Venues that use Encore as a service, in three tiers: one Enterprise tenant in its own silo (`venue-chain-a`); Professional tenants in Kubernetes cells, with a database per tenant; and about 500 self-service venues pooled in serverless cells of about 50 each | [Architecture book, 14.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#two-planes-three-models); Chapters 1, 3 and 5 |
| **Regions** | EU-1 and EU-2. Reads are served from both; writes go to each event's home region. Each seat-ledger shard is a five-member consensus group, with two replicas in each of the two regions and a witness in EU-3, and a write commits once a majority holds it. The result is a recovery point objective (RPO) of zero and a recovery time objective (RTO) of minutes | [Architecture book, 11.5](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#surviving-a-region); Chapter 6 |
| **Golden path** | The platform's supported route from a service template to production, which provisions the infrastructure, registers the service and gives it a scorecard | [Architecture book, 15.2](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#golden-paths-on-a-self-service-control-plane); Chapters 3 and 4 |
| **Curtain-up** | The self-service workflow that pre-warms the purchase path on the morning of an on-sale | [Architecture book, 11.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-spectrum-priced-in-responsibility); Chapters 2 and 3; Operations book, Chapter 3 |
| **Readiness review and freeze** | The Wednesday review held before a big on-sale, followed by a freeze on non-emergency changes to the purchase path that runs from two hours before the on-sale until it ends | [Architecture book, 11.3](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#from-is-it-up-to-why-is-it-weird); Chapters 2 and 4; Operations book, Chapter 6 |
| **Reliability team** | Ten engineers in the platform group, led by a staff site reliability engineer (SRE). They own the SLO tooling, the incident process, readiness reviews and game days | [Architecture book, 15.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#cognitive-load-is-the-resource-being-architected); Chapters 1 and 5; Operations book, Chapter 7 |
| **Published numbers** | The seat map loads within 800 ms for 99.9% of requests over 28 days. Tenant service level objectives (SLOs) are 99.9% for self-service and 99.95% for Professional. Blended cost per ticket drifted from €0.11 to €0.19 and is now about €0.16, of which about €0.12 is variable | [Architecture book, 11.3](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#from-is-it-up-to-why-is-it-weird); Chapters 1 and 2 |
| **Cue** | The operations agent, working through the internal Model Context Protocol (MCP) server. It is read-only by default. It may invoke only the actions in a catalog that the platform enforces, and each invocation gets a five-minute token scoped to that one action. All but two actions need a person's approval; the two exceptions are raising Catalog's autoscaler floor and holding a node in the upgrade reconciler. During the on-sale freeze, its write scopes on the purchase path are withdrawn | [Architecture book, 12.3](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-loop-that-acts); Operations book, Chapters 3 and 5 |
| **AI gateway** | The router for the assistant's and the agent's model calls. It sends about 80% of them to a model that costs a tenth as much | [Architecture book, 12.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#121-ai-in-the-system-topology); Chapters 2 and 8 |
| **Support assistant and refund agent** | Two AI components: a retrieval-augmented assistant that answers fans, and an agent that approves refunds under €80 when showtime is more than 48 h away and sends every other case to a person | [Architecture book, 12.1](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#121-ai-in-the-system-topology); Chapter 8 |
| **The Vesper Hale on-sale** | The on-sale for a fictional artist's 12-date European tour: about 1.1 million fans in the Gate, 47 minutes of degraded checkout, and no seat sold twice | Chapter 1; Operations book, Chapters 5 and 6 |

*Figure A.1 — Encore at a glance. The Architecture book explains why each part was designed as it was; the named chapter shows what it takes to run.*
